⏺ This repo is a take-home exercise. You get a small pipeline: data goes in, an
  LLM makes a decision, the decision is scored, and you view the results. The
  only piece you're asked to really build is triage_submission(...) in core.py.
  Everything else is there to run and score it.

  The problem

  A surgical center needs to know if a patient is cleared for surgery. Each case
  is a JSON "submission package" with the patient, the procedure, vitals, labs,
  medications, conditions and free-text clinical documents. The function must
  return:

  - decision: READY, NEEDS_FOLLOW_UP or NOT_CLEARED
  - issues[]: each one has a category (one of 5 fixed values) and evidence. The
    evidence format is up to you.
  - explanation: free text

  The rules live in the system prompt at core.py:14-53 (core.py#L14-L53):

  ┌─────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────┬─────────────────┐
  │      Rule       │                Check                │   If it fails   │
  ├─────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────┤
  │ 1.              │ H&P done within 30 days of surgery, │ NEEDS_FOLLOW_UP │
  │ Documentation   │  plus a signed consent              │                 │
  ├─────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────┤
  │                 │ LOW/MODERATE risk: CBC within 30    │                 │
  │ 2. Testing      │ days. HIGH risk: CBC and CMP within │ NEEDS_FOLLOW_UP │
  │                 │  14 days. Only the latest result    │                 │
  │                 │ counts.                             │                 │
  ├─────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────┤
  │ 3.              │ On a blood thinner means a clear    │                 │
  │ Anticoagulation │ perioperative plan must be          │ NEEDS_FOLLOW_UP │
  │                 │ documented                          │                 │
  ├─────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────┤
  │ 4. Acute safety │ Latest BP ≥180 systolic or ≥110     │ NOT_CLEARED     │
  │                 │ diastolic, or temperature >100.4°F  │                 │
  ├─────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────┤
  │ Fallback        │ A field needed for a rule is        │ NEEDS_FOLLOW_UP │
  │                 │ missing                             │                 │
  └─────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────┴─────────────────┘

  The files

  core.py: the core logic

  1. Policy prompt (BASELINE_SYSTEM_PROMPT): the rules above, written out for
     the LLM.
  2. Type aliases: Decision, ProcedureRisk, and IssueCategory
     (REQUIRED_DOCUMENTATION, REQUIRED_TESTING, ANTICOAGULATION_MANAGEMENT,
     ACUTE_SAFETY_EXCLUSION, MISSING_REQUIRED_DATA).
  3. Pydantic input models (PatientSubmission and its children): these describe
     the input shape. Every field is optional. Vital is a union of
     blood-pressure, temperature and generic vitals.
  4. Output model (TriageOutput): decision, issues and explanation.
     triage_output_json_schema() turns it into a JSON Schema that is sent to
     OpenAI.
  5. build_user_prompt: dumps the submission as JSON with an instruction line in
     front.
  6. triage_submission, the current naive baseline:
     - validates the input through PatientSubmission, then dumps it back to a
       dict
     - creates a new OpenAI() client on every call
     - makes one call to client.responses.create with the policy as instructions
       and a JSON-schema output format (strict: False)
     - parses response.output_text into TriageOutput

     The README says this "intentionally does not follow some best practices".
     Visible examples: a new client on every call, strict: False, no
     temperature/seed setting, no retries or error handling, and the LLM does
     all the date math and threshold comparisons itself.

  run_baseline.py: batch runner (make baseline)

  - Reads data/patients_sample_50.jsonl and calls triage_submission on each
    case, one after another.
  - Writes one row per case to data/baseline_outputs.jsonl: record_index,
    case_id, submission, model, and either output or error. Exceptions are
    caught and saved, so one failure doesn't stop the run.
  - --max-records N limits the run to the first N cases, which is handy for
    cheap tests.

  run_evals.py: scoring (make evals, make determinism)

  It has two modes.

  Eval mode (default):
  - Loads the labeled cases and the baseline outputs, and matches them up by
    record_index.
  - Scores each case locally on 3 pass/fail metrics, all weighted the same:
    a. json_schema_valid: the output parses (decision and issue categories must
       be valid enum values)
    b. decision_match_oracle: the decision equals the human label
    c. issue_categories_match_oracle: the set of issue categories exactly equals
       the label's set. Duplicates collapse and order doesn't matter, but one
       extra or one missing category means a fail.
  - Each case scores 0, 33, 67 or 100. The primary score is the average across
    cases.
  - It also uploads the same pass/fail data to the OpenAI Evals API as
    string_check criteria, waits for the run to finish, and saves the run's URL
    and results. That part only mirrors the local scores. The score you see
    comes from the local calculation.
  - Writes data/eval_report.json, which includes a full record for each case.

  Determinism mode (--determinism):
  - Runs triage_submission 10 times on one case (record 0 by default).
  - Reports three numbers: how often the most common decision came back
    (decision stability), how often the JSON was valid, and how often the output
    matched exactly.

  view_report.py: terminal UI (make report)

  A Textual (https://textual.textualize.io/) app that reads eval_report.json:
  - The top shows the overall score and a table of pass rates per metric.
  - The left side lists cases with their score and which metrics failed.
  - The right side has two panes: the result (your decision vs. the label, the
    labeler's rationale, your issues and explanation) and the raw submission
    JSON.
  - Pressing f on a metric row shows only the cases that failed that metric.

  It shows evidence whatever its shape: a string, or an object/list turned into
  JSON.

  tests/test_triage_submission.py (make test)

  Three unit tests. They swap out the openai module with a mock, so no network
  call happens:
  1. The output is parsed correctly, and the request has the right model,
     instructions and schema. It also asserts strict is False.
  2. The user prompt contains the validated submission JSON.
  3. An invalid decision such as "MAYBE" raises ValidationError.

  These tests are tied closely to the current implementation. If you change how
  the API is called (for example strict: True or a different prompt layout),
  they will fail and need updating.

  Other files

  - Makefile: shortcuts for the commands above. MODEL, INPUT and the other paths
    can be overridden. make score prints the primary score.
  - init.py (__init__.py): a one-line package docstring and nothing else.
  - .gitignore: ignores the three generated output files.

  The data

  The file has 50 cases, each with case_id, submission and label (decision,
  categories, and a free-text rationale from the human labeler).

  - Decisions: 25 NEEDS_FOLLOW_UP, 17 READY, 8 NOT_CLEARED.
  - Procedure risk: 26 LOW, 14 MODERATE, 8 HIGH, and 2 missing (which should
    trigger the missing-data rule).

  The data is deliberately messy in ways that make simple keyword matching
  unreliable:
  - Document types use many names for the same thing. An H&P shows up as "H&P",
    "Admission H&P", "Clinic H&P", "Preoperative History & Physical (H&P)" and
    more. Consents show up as "Surgery Consent (scanned)", "Procedure Consent
    Form" and others. Many documents are irrelevant: nutrition, PT, radiology,
    portal messages.
  - Lab codes vary. A CBC appears as CBC and LAB-CBC under different display
    names. Extra labs like BMP, TSH and PT-INR are distractors, and a BMP is not
    a CMP.
  - Important facts often sit only in free-text notes. In the first case, the
    patient takes Xarelto (an anticoagulant), but it is not in the medication
    list. Only a nursing note mentions it, and the anticoagulation plan is in a
    cardiology consult. That case also has an older LAB-CBC next to a newer CBC,
    which tests the "use the most recent result" rule.

  One subtle behavior: PatientSubmission silently drops any fields it doesn't
  define. I checked that the vitals union parses temperatures correctly, so
  value_f survives. But if real data ever carries extra fields, such as a
  medication dose, the model never sees them.

  How the pieces connect

  patients_sample_50.jsonl
          │  run_baseline.py  → triage_submission() → OpenAI
          ▼
  baseline_outputs.jsonl
          │  run_evals.py  (local scoring + OpenAI Evals copy)
          ▼
  eval_report.json  ──► view_report.py (terminal UI)

  To run it you need uv and the OPENAI_API_KEY environment variable set. Every
  baseline run makes 50 paid API calls, and every determinism run makes 10.

⏺ You already have a report from a previous run (data/eval_report.json), so I
  analyzed it without making any new API calls. Below is what the assignment
  asks for, why the score is where it is, and a proposed plan.

  What you're meant to do

  From the PDF and README, you need to hand in four things:

  1. A better triage_submission(...). It takes one patient package and returns:
     - decision: READY, NEEDS_FOLLOW_UP or NOT_CLEARED
     - issues[]: each issue has a category (one of the 5 fixed values) plus
       evidence you design yourself. A pre-op nurse checks every non-READY case
       and some READY ones, so the evidence should let them confirm the result
       quickly (which document or lab, the date, the exact quote, the rule
       applied).
     - explanation
  2. Build and run instructions, if they differ from the starter repo.
  3. A short write-up of your approach, design decisions and assumptions.
  4. An export of your coding session (/export in Claude Code).

  They say outright that a perfect score matters less than engineering judgment.
  They also say the starter code "intentionally does not follow some best
  practices" with the OpenAI API, so fixing those is part of the task.

  What "~70%" actually means

  The number is 74.67%, and it isn't "74% of cases pass". Each case is scored on
  3 checks, and the 74.67% is the average:

  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────┬──────────────┐
  │                       Check                       │  Pass rate   │
  ├───────────────────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────┤
  │ Output is valid JSON matching the schema          │ 100% (50/50) │
  ├───────────────────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────┤
  │ Decision matches the human label                  │ 66% (33/50)  │
  ├───────────────────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────┤
  │ Set of issue categories matches the label exactly │ 58% (29/50)  │
  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────┴──────────────┘

  Only 21 of 50 cases pass all three checks.

  make test is a separate thing: 3 unit tests with a mocked OpenAI client. They
  don't affect this score.

  Decisions: what the label said vs. what the model returned

  ┌──────────────────────┬───────┬─────────────────┬─────────────┐
  │  Label ↓ / Model →   │ READY │ NEEDS_FOLLOW_UP │ NOT_CLEARED │
  ├──────────────────────┼───────┼─────────────────┼─────────────┤
  │ READY (17)           │ 5     │ 11              │ 1           │
  ├──────────────────────┼───────┼─────────────────┼─────────────┤
  │ NEEDS_FOLLOW_UP (25) │ 1     │ 24              │ 0           │
  ├──────────────────────┼───────┼─────────────────┼─────────────┤
  │ NOT_CLEARED (8)      │ 0     │ 4               │ 4           │
  └──────────────────────┴───────┴─────────────────┴─────────────┘

  The baseline defaults to NEEDS_FOLLOW_UP far too often. It gets only 5 of 17
  READY cases right, and half of the NOT_CLEARED cases.

  Why the failing cases fail

  I read the labeler's rationale for each of the 29 failing cases. Almost every
  failure fits one of these patterns:

  A. Medication mistakes (about 10 cases).
  - Aspirin and clopidogrel are antiplatelets, not anticoagulants, so they need
    no plan. The model flagged them anyway (00002, 00025, 00041, 00049).
  - It missed valid plans written with brand names like Xarelto or Eliquis, or
    plans found only in a consult note (00000, 00003, 00037).
  - It treated inactive warfarin as active (00043).
  - In the other direction, it accepted a plan with no resume instructions
    (00020), which should fail.
  - It also missed a medication whose active status is unknown (00033), which
    should be MISSING_REQUIRED_DATA.

  B. Vitals that appear only in note text (5 cases). The latest BP or
  temperature is often inside a nursing note, not the structured vitals list,
  and sometimes in Celsius.
  - The model missed a fever of 38.6 °C (00002), a BP of 157/117 (00005, 00047),
    and a recheck still at 181 (00032). These should be NOT_CLEARED.
  - The reverse also happens: an addendum recheck at 137/78 overrides an earlier
    189/112 (00016), but the model returned NOT_CLEARED anyway.

  C. Missing data labeled with the wrong category (6 cases).
  - No temperature anywhere should be flagged as MISSING_REQUIRED_DATA. The
    model never does this (00009, 00011, 00013, 00021).
  - A blank procedure date should produce only MISSING_REQUIRED_DATA. The model
    instead cascades it into documentation and testing failures (00011, 00044).

  D. Document details it got wrong (about 8 cases).
  - A consent that was sent but never signed (00028, 00031), or signed for the
    wrong procedure (00024), was not caught.
  - A newer signed consent replaces an older pending one (00007, 00049).
  - A lab marked "entered-in-error" should be skipped (00004, 00041).
  - An H&P titled with a typo ("History and Pyhsical") still counts (00045).
  - An old wellness visit is not the H&P that matters (00034).

  E. Exact date and threshold cutoffs.
  - An H&P 31 days before surgery fails. A CBC 15 days before a high-risk case
    fails.
  - A diastolic of exactly 110 means NOT_CLEARED.
  - The model also invented extra issues on a case with only one real problem
    (00014).

  The pattern: the model is unreliable at reading messy documents and also at
  applying rules precisely (date math, thresholds, picking the latest result). A
  single prompt asks it to do both at once.

  Proposed plan

  Split the work: the LLM extracts facts, plain Python applies the rules.

  1. Extraction (LLM, structured output). Have the model return facts, not a
     decision. Examples: the H&P documents found (date, is it a real pre-op H&P,
     quote), consents (signed by the patient? which procedure? supersedes
     another?), anticoagulants (actually an anticoagulant? active? plan covers
     before and after?), and vitals found in notes (value, unit, time, quote).
     Every fact carries a doc_id and a short quote.
  2. Rules engine (deterministic Python). Applies Rules 1–4 using dates computed
     in code, exact thresholds (>=180, >=110, >100.4), Celsius-to-Fahrenheit
     conversion, latest-value selection, skipping "entered-in-error" results,
     and the missing-data conventions (a blank procedure date gives only
     MISSING_REQUIRED_DATA). Lab code matching (CBC vs LAB-CBC, BMP is not CMP)
     can also live in code.
  3. Evidence packet. Each issue includes the rule, the source (doc_id or lab
     id), the date, the quote, and the computed value (for example "31 days >
     30"). A nurse can check it in seconds.
  4. API fixes. Reuse one client, use strict: True schemas, temperature=0 and a
     fixed seed, add retries and timeouts, and process cases in parallel. Then
     update the unit tests that currently assert the old behavior.
  5. Iterate cheaply. Use --max-records, re-score, and work through the failure
     groups above. Run make determinism to show the result is stable.
  6. Write-up. Explain the architecture, the assumptions taken from the label
     rationales (antiplatelets aren't anticoagulants, a missing temperature
     counts as missing data, a recheck overrides an earlier reading), and the
     remaining known misses.

  Because the rules engine is plain Python, it can be unit-tested without
  calling the API at all. That is the kind of engineering judgment they say they
  want to see.

  To reproduce this breakdown, run python3
  /private/tmp/claude-501/-Users-annanguyen-cadence-takehome/5896d719-901a-4c2d-
  a7e9-ba9478a7312c/scratchpad/analyze.py data/eval_report.json. make report
  shows the same cases in the terminal UI.

  If this direction works for you, the next step is to define the extraction
  schema and the rules module.

❯ what do you mean by "evidence I design myself" ? am i essentially just adding
  onto the prompt passed to AI with more explicit detail or is there specific
  code that I need to implement as well?

⏺ It's mostly code, not prompt wording. "Evidence" is a data design question:
  what fields each issue in the output carries, and how those fields get filled
  in.

  What exists today

  In core.py:158-161 (core.py#L158-L161), an issue is:

  class TriageIssue(BaseModel):
      category: IssueCategory
      evidence: str

  evidence is a single free-text string that the LLM writes, so you get
  something like:

  {"category": "REQUIRED_DOCUMENTATION", "evidence": "H&P appears to be
  outdated."}

  A nurse can't check that quickly. Which H&P? What date? Outdated by how much?
  And the model may have made it up.

  What "design it yourself" means

  The PDF leaves the shape of each issue open ("...": "your evidence design
  here"). The automated scorer only looks at category, but the reviewers will
  judge whether a nurse could verify the decision without re-reading the whole
  chart. You decide what that looks like, for example:

  {
    "category": "REQUIRED_DOCUMENTATION",
    "rule": "Rule 1: H&P within 30 days of procedure",
    "finding": "Most recent H&P is 31 days before procedure (limit 30)",
    "source": {"kind": "document", "id": "c92f23f5-...", "type": "History and
  Physical", "date": "2026-02-12"},
    "quote": "HISTORY AND PHYSICAL: pre-op evaluation complete...",
    "computed": {"procedure_date": "2026-03-15", "days_before": 31,
  "limit_days": 30}
  }

  Every claim points to a specific document or lab id, a date, and the exact
  text it came from, plus the arithmetic that was done.

  Prompt or code?

  Mostly code. There are three parts:

  1. Schema (code). Replace evidence: str with a structured Pydantic model like
     the one above. This is a short change in core.py.
  2. Where the values come from (code plus prompt). This is the important part.
     - Fragile: ask the LLM to fill in those fields directly. You get the right
       shape, but dates, day counts and ids can still be wrong or invented.
     - Robust: the LLM only extracts facts from the notes, each with a doc_id
       and a quote. Python then computes days_before, compares against limits,
       and builds the issue and its evidence itself. Code can also check that
       each quote really appears in the cited document, which catches
       hallucinations.
  3. Prompt (smaller part). The prompt changes from "decide
     READY/NEEDS_FOLLOW_UP/NOT_CLEARED" to "find these specific facts and quote
     where you found them". The policy and its thresholds move out of the prompt
     and into code.

  Two side benefits: issues produced by code can't come back with invented
  categories, which was one cause of the 58% category score. And READY cases can
  include a short "checks passed" list with the same kind of evidence, since
  nurses spot-check those too.

  So, in summary: a schema change, a new extraction prompt and schema, and a
  rules module in Python that produces the decision and the evidence. Want me to
  sketch the extraction schema and evidence model first, so you can react to
  the shape before any logic is written?

❯ Got it, can you re-explain why exactly baseline fails again? Can you read the
  eval report again and see if you recognize any patterns for why things might
  not pass or are going wrong?

  the failing cases, not just the labeler's rationale. That shows where its
  reasoning goes wrong.

⏺ Reading the model's own output shows a clearer picture than the labels alone.
  Most failures aren't about the model missing information. It often finds the
  right facts and then makes the wrong decision from them. There are six
  patterns, roughly in order of how many cases they cost.

  1. The decision doesn't follow from the model's own reasoning (about 8 cases)

  This is the biggest pattern:

  - It finds no problems, then says NEEDS_FOLLOW_UP anyway. In 00004, 00007,
    00025, 00037 and 00043, the explanation walks through every rule, says each
    one passes, and lists zero issues, yet the decision is NEEDS_FOLLOW_UP.
  - It finds a safety exclusion, then doesn't return NOT_CLEARED. In 00005 and
    00047 it flags ACUTE_SAFETY_EXCLUSION (diastolic 117) but returns
    NEEDS_FOLLOW_UP. In 00047 the explanation even says "the correct final
    decision should be NOT_CLEARED", but the decision field had already been
    written.
  - It finds a valid plan, then invents a reason to hold. In 00003 it found the
    Eliquis plan, then added "requires follow-up to confirm adherence", which is
    not a policy rule.

  Why: in core.py:163-168 (core.py#L163-L168), TriageOutput lists decision
  first, then issues, then explanation. The model writes JSON top to bottom, so
  it commits to the decision before doing any reasoning and then explains
  afterward. When it's unsure it falls back to the "safe" NEEDS_FOLLOW_UP. That
  also explains the earlier finding that READY is right only 5 of 17 times.

  2. Wrong date arithmetic (at least 3 cases)

  The model counts days itself and makes mistakes:

  ┌────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────┬─────────────────────┐
  │      Case      │           What it said           │      Actually       │
  ├────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ 00014          │ H&P 02/12 → surgery 03/15 is     │ 31 days, fails      │
  │                │ "within 30 days"                 │                     │
  ├────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ 00021          │ CBC 24 days out is "outside the  │ 24 < 30, passes     │
  │                │ 30-day window"                   │                     │
  ├────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ 00007, 00005,  │ 26, 13 and 16 days               │ 24, 12 and 15 (off  │
  │ 00009          │                                  │ by 1–2)             │
  └────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────┴─────────────────────┘

  The first two changed the outcome. The others were lucky. With limits this
  tight (30 vs. 31, 14 vs. 15), an off-by-one error is a wrong answer.

  3. Requirements the policy doesn't contain (about 7 cases)

  - Aspirin treated as an anticoagulant (00002, 00024, 00041, 00049). In 00041
    it even writes "aspirin is an antiplatelet... not considered a strong
    anticoagulant" and still flags it.
  - Made-up lab requirements. In 00034 and 00045 it marks a CBC as a problem
    because "lab result values are not provided". The policy only cares that a
    CBC exists and is within the date window.
  - Plans demanded for patients not on anticoagulants (00014, 00044).

  The prompt says not to add criteria, but nothing enforces it.

  4. The issues list is used inconsistently (about 6 cases)

  - Checks that passed are listed as issues. 00014 lists four issues, three of
    which say things like "CBC within required window" and "No vital signs
    show...". Each one counts as an extra category and fails the category check.
  - The first problem ends the search. In 00028 and 00031 it finds the safety
    exclusion, writes a one-line explanation, and never checks the consent,
    which was unsigned in both. The labels expect every problem listed, even
    when the result is NOT_CLEARED.
  - One missing field is reported as several issues. In 00011 and 00044 a blank
    procedure date turns into REQUIRED_DOCUMENTATION twice plus
    REQUIRED_TESTING. The labels expect a single MISSING_REQUIRED_DATA.

  5. Labeling conventions the prompt never mentions (about 6 cases)

  These are rules the human labeler applied consistently, but the prompt never
  states them, so the model can't guess them:

  - No temperature anywhere means MISSING_REQUIRED_DATA. The model never flags
    this (00009, 00011, 00013, 00021).
  - A blank procedure date gives only MISSING_REQUIRED_DATA, not documentation
    or testing failures (00011, 00044).
  - An anticoagulant with unknown active status means MISSING_REQUIRED_DATA, not
    an anticoagulation issue (00033).
  - A plan with no restart instructions is incomplete (00020). The model
    accepted a plan that only covered stopping the drug.

  6. Missed or misread vitals in note text (3 cases)

  - 00002: a fever of 38.6 °C in a nursing note was missed completely.
  - 00032: says "vital signs are acceptable", but the note has 196/110 with a
    recheck at 181/98.
  - 00016: used 189/112 and missed the addendum recheck of 137/78 taken 24
    minutes later.

  The data clearly tests this on purpose: structured vitals look normal, a later
  note is abnormal, sometimes in Celsius, sometimes with a recheck.

  What this means for the fix

  ┌────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────┬─────────────────────┐
  │      Pattern       │ Better prompting fixes it?  │   Code fixes it?    │
  ├────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ 1. Decision        │ Partly (put explanation     │ Fully: derive the   │
  │ doesn't follow the │ first)                      │ decision from the   │
  │  reasoning         │                             │ issues in code      │
  ├────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ 2. Date arithmetic │ No                          │ Fully: compute      │
  │                    │                             │ dates in Python     │
  ├────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ 3. Invented        │ Partly                      │ Mostly: only code   │
  │ requirements       │                             │ can create issues   │
  ├────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ 4. Messy issues    │                             │ Fully: code builds  │
  │ list               │ Partly                      │ the list, one per   │
  │                    │                             │ failed rule         │
  ├────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ 5. Unstated        │ Yes, if spelled out         │ Fully: encode them  │
  │ conventions        │                             │ as rules            │
  ├────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ 6. Vitals in note  │ Yes: targeted extraction    │ Code picks the      │
  │ text               │ ("find all BP/temp readings │ latest and applies  │
  │                    │  in notes with timestamps") │ the thresholds      │
  └────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────┴─────────────────────┘

  Only pattern 6, and part of the medication classification, really needs the
  LLM. Everything else is logic the LLM currently does badly and Python does
  exactly. That supports the split from before: the LLM extracts facts (vitals
  in notes, which drugs are anticoagulants, whether a plan covers before and
  after, whether a consent is signed and for which procedure), and code decides.

  One cheap experiment if you want to measure the difference: moving explanation
  above decision in the schema should recover several pattern-1 cases on its
  own. That's useful as a "before" data point for your write-up.

⏺ Here's the plan. I checked the data first, so the extraction schema below
  matches what's actually in the 50 cases. Nothing has been changed in the repo
  yet.

  What I checked in the data:
  - Already structured, no LLM needed: labs (including the entered-in-error
    status), procedure date and risk, and the structured vitals and medication
    list.
  - Only in free text, LLM needed:
    - which document is the real H&P (one is titled "History and Pyhsical")
    - whether a consent is signed by the patient, and for which procedure
    - anticoagulants the patient reports in a note but that aren't on the
      medication list
    - perioperative anticoagulation plans
    - vitals recorded only in notes (Celsius readings, rechecks)
  - Size: at most 8 documents and about 7.5k characters per case, so one
    extraction call per case is plenty.

  Plan: LLM extracts facts, Python makes the decision

  Architecture

  submission ──► extraction.py (LLM) ──► ExtractedFacts (structured JSON +
  quotes)
                                                │
  submission (structured fields) ───────────────┤
                                                ▼
                                     rules.py (plain Python)
                                                │
                                                ▼
                       TriageOutput (decision + issues + evidence)

  The LLM only answers questions like "what does this document say?" It never
  sees the policy thresholds and never produces a decision or a category.

  Part 1: Extraction (extraction.py)

  One LLM call per submission. It gets the documents (with doc_id, type, date,
  text), the scheduled procedure_type, and the medication list.

  What it returns (strict JSON schema). Every fact includes doc_id and a
  word-for-word quote:

  Fact: hp_documents[]
  Fields: doc_id, date, is_preop_hp (vs. a wellness or establish-care visit),
  quote
  Why it's needed: Titles are unreliable (typos, "Admission H&P" for an old
  visit)
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Fact: consents[]
  Fields: doc_id, date, signed_by_patient (yes / no / pending), procedure_named,

  matches_scheduled_procedure, quote
  Why it's needed: Unsigned or pending consents, consent for the wrong
  procedure,
  a newer consent replacing an older one
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Fact: medication_mentions[]
  Fields: name as written, doc_id, status (taking / stopped / uncertain), quote
  Why it's needed: Anticoagulants mentioned only in notes (00000, 00027, 00038),

  or with unclear status (00033)
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Fact: anticoag_plans[]
  Fields: doc_id, date, drug as written, pre_op_instruction quote or null,
  post_op_instruction quote or null, quote
  Why it's needed: Plans must cover both before and after surgery (00020, 00023)
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Fact: note_vitals[]
  Fields: doc_id, kind (bp / temp), systolic, diastolic, value, unit (F/C),
  measured_at (time if stated), quote
  Why it's needed: Fevers in Celsius, rechecks, readings newer than the
  structured vitals

  What it does not do: decide whether something is an anticoagulant, compute
  days, convert units, compare against thresholds, or pick the "latest" reading.
  Those are all Python.

  API fixes: one shared client, strict: True structured outputs, temperature=0,
  a fixed seed, a timeout, and retries.

  Quote check in code: each quote is checked as a substring of the cited
  document (with whitespace normalized). Facts that fail the check are flagged
  so a nurse sees them; see question 3.

  Part 2: Rule checks (rules.py, plain Python)

  Small helpers first: date difference in days, Celsius to Fahrenheit, lab-code
  mapping (CBC/LAB-CBC → CBC, CMP/LAB-CMP → CMP, BMP never counts as CMP), and a
  brand-to-generic anticoagulant table (warfarin/Coumadin, apixaban/Eliquis,
  rivaroxaban/Xarelto, dabigatran/Pradaxa, edoxaban/Savaysa, enoxaparin,
  heparin, …). Aspirin and clopidogrel are deliberately not on the list.

  Rule: Missing data
  Logic: No procedure date → flag and skip all date-window checks. No risk level

  → flag and skip the lab rule. No temperature or no BP anywhere (structured or
   notes) → flag. Anticoagulant with uncertain status → flag.
  Category: MISSING_REQUIRED_DATA
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Rule: 1. Documentation
  Logic: Most recent document with is_preop_hp: 0 ≤ days ≤ 30 (inclusive; 30
  passes, 31 fails, per 00012 and 00014). Most recent consent must be signed by
   the patient and match the scheduled procedure (newest consent wins).
  Category: REQUIRED_DOCUMENTATION
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Rule: 2. Testing
  Logic: Drop entered-in-error results. Take the most recent result per test and

  check days ≤ 30 (LOW/MODERATE) or ≤ 14 for both CBC and CMP (HIGH).
  Category: REQUIRED_TESTING
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Rule: 3. Anticoagulation
  Logic: Combine the structured med list and note mentions, then decide who is
  currently on an anticoagulant. For each one, a plan for the same drug (00018:
   a warfarin plan doesn't cover apixaban) must include both pre-op and post-op
   instructions.
  Category: ANTICOAGULATION_MANAGEMENT
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Rule: 4. Safety
  Logic: Combine structured and note vitals and take the latest BP and latest
  temperature separately. Thresholds: systolic ≥ 180, diastolic ≥ 110, temp_F >
   100.4.
  Category: ACUTE_SAFETY_EXCLUSION

  Decision (code only): any safety exclusion → NOT_CLEARED; otherwise any issue
  → NEEDS_FOLLOW_UP; otherwise READY. Every rule always runs, so a NOT_CLEARED
  case still reports an unsigned consent (00028, 00031).

  Output and evidence

  {
    "decision": "NEEDS_FOLLOW_UP",
    "issues": [{
      "category": "REQUIRED_DOCUMENTATION",
      "rule": "Rule 1: H&P within 30 days",
      "finding": "Most recent pre-op H&P is 31 days before procedure (limit
  30)",
      "sources": [{"kind": "document", "id": "c92f…", "date": "2026-02-12",
  "quote": "…", "quote_verified": true}],
      "computed": {"procedure_date": "2026-03-15", "days_before": 31,
  "limit_days": 30}
    }],
    "checks_passed": [ /* same shape, for nurses spot-checking READY cases */ ],
    "explanation": "Generated from issues and passed checks by a template, not
  by the LLM"
  }

  The scorer only reads decision and issues[].category and accepts extra fields,
  so this schema is compatible with it.

  Files

  - core.py: keeps the shared types. TriageOutput and the issue model gain the
    evidence fields. triage_submission(submission, *, model) keeps the same
    signature and becomes: extract → apply rules → build output.
  - extraction.py (new): prompt, extraction schema, OpenAI call, quote check.
  - rules.py (new): pure functions, no I/O, no LLM.
  - tests/test_rules.py (new): hand-built facts → expected issues. Covers the
    boundary cases (30/31 days, 14/15 days, diastolic exactly 110, 37.9 °C =
    100.2 °F passes, entered-in-error labs, aspirin only, plan with no resume,
    wrong-drug plan, missing date/risk/temperature). No API calls.
  - tests/test_triage_submission.py: update it, since it currently asserts the
    old prompt and strict: False.
  - WRITEUP.md (new): approach, assumptions, known gaps.

  Build order (you can review after each step)

  1. Schemas: the extraction model and the evidence/output model.
  2. rules.py plus its unit tests, verified without any API calls.
  3. extraction.py, run on about 5 cases with --max-records, raw extractions
     inspected by hand.
  4. Wire both into triage_submission and fix the existing tests.
  5. Full make baseline and make evals, then a failure-analysis loop using the
     same scripts as before.
  6. make determinism, then the write-up.

  Decisions for you (my recommendation first)

  1. Is a drug an anticoagulant? A hardcoded list in Python (recommended;
     deterministic and testable). Alternative: let the LLM classify it.
  2. Does the consent match the scheduled procedure? Let the LLM report
     matches_scheduled_procedure with the quoted procedure name (recommended;
     "CABG" vs. "coronary artery bypass grafting" is hard to match in code).
     Alternative: fuzzy matching in Python.
  3. A quote that isn't found in its document: keep the fact and mark
     quote_verified: false in the evidence (recommended). Alternative: drop the
     fact, which could hide a real fever.
  4. Two vitals on the same day with no time to tell them apart: the more severe
     reading wins (recommended, the cautious choice). Alternative: the reading
     from the later document wins.
  5. Cache extractions on disk, keyed by a hash of submission, prompt and model,
     so iterating on the rules costs nothing and reruns are deterministic
     (recommended). Alternative: no cache.
  6. Labs only from the structured labs list (recommended; no label relies on a
     lab found only in a note). Alternative: also extract labs from notes.

  Tell me your choices on these six, or anything you want to change, and I'll
  start with step 1.

❯ also one more requirement for the extraction schema: every extracted fact

  Plan: LLM extracts facts, Python decides (v2)

  Architecture

  submission ──► extraction.py (LLM) ──► raw facts (each with doc_id + quote)
                                               │

  The LLM never sees thresholds and never produces a decision or a category.

  Part 1: Extraction (extraction.py)

  One call per submission. The input is the documents (doc_id, type, date,
  text), the scheduled procedure_type, and the medication list as context. The
  output uses a strict JSON schema.

  [new] Every fact has doc_id and quote, both required in the schema. The prompt
  says quotes must be copied word for word, be short (one sentence or less),
  and contain the value being extracted.

  ┌───────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │         Fact          │           Fields (plus doc_id, quote)            │
  ├───────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ hp_documents[]        │ date, is_preop_hp                                │
  ├───────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ consents[]            │ date, signed_by_patient (yes / no / pending),    │

  ├───────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────┤

  ├───────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────┤

  │                       │ unit (F/C), measured_at                          │
  └───────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  [new] Two-part plans: the pre-op and post-op instructions are often in
  different sentences, so each one gets its own quote. Each quote is verified
  separately. If one fails, only that half is dropped, and the plan then counts
  as incomplete under Rule 3.

  [new] The LLM reads only documents. Labs, structured vitals, the medication
  list and procedure fields are read directly by code. They don't need quote
  checks because nothing was extracted from free text.

  API fixes: one shared client, strict: True, temperature=0, a fixed seed, a
  timeout, retries.

  [new] Part 1b: Quote verification (verify.py, plain Python)

  For each extracted fact (or each quote, for the two-part plans):

  1. doc_id must be one of the submission's documents. If not → discard.
  2. The quote must not be empty or whitespace only. If it is → discard.
  3. Normalize the quote and the document text: Unicode NFKC (so curly quotes
     and similar characters match), casefold(), and remove all whitespace. If
     the normalized quote isn't a substring of the normalized text → discard.
  4. (Recommended, see decision A) Value check: the numbers the fact claims
     (systolic, diastolic, temperature value) must appear in the quote. A real
     quote paired with an invented number is also discarded.

  What happens to a discarded fact:
  - It is removed before the rules run, so the rules behave as if it was never
    found. A dropped consent means no consent → REQUIRED_DOCUMENTATION. A
    dropped plan → ANTICOAGULATION_MANAGEMENT.
  - And it produces a MISSING_REQUIRED_DATA issue whose evidence includes the
    fact as extracted, the doc_id, the quote that wasn't found, and the reason.
    This guarantees at least NEEDS_FOLLOW_UP, even when dropping the fact would
    otherwise have made the case look better (for example a dropped "patient
    stopped warfarin" mention, or a dropped high BP reading).
  - It never lowers a decision: a verified safety exclusion still gives
    NOT_CLEARED.

  Trade-off to watch: that extra MISSING_REQUIRED_DATA category will fail the
  category check for that case whenever the label doesn't include it. That's the
  right call for safety. I'll report how many facts get discarded per run, so
  we can tell whether it's rare or whether the prompt needs fixing.

  Part 2: Rule checks (rules.py, plain Python)

  These are unchanged from v1 and use only verified facts plus structured data.

  Rule: Missing data
  Logic: No procedure date → flag and skip window checks. No risk level → flag
  and skip labs. No temperature or no BP anywhere → flag. Anticoagulant with
  uncertain status → flag. [new] Each discarded fact → flag.
  Category: MISSING_REQUIRED_DATA
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Rule: 1. Documentation
  Logic: Most recent is_preop_hp, 0 ≤ days ≤ 30. Newest consent is signed by the

  patient and matches the procedure.
  Category: REQUIRED_DOCUMENTATION
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Rule: 2. Testing
  Logic: Drop entered-in-error, take the latest result per test, check ≤30 days
  (LOW/MOD) or ≤14 days for both CBC and CMP (HIGH).
  Category: REQUIRED_TESTING
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Rule: 3. Anticoagulation
  Logic: Hardcoded anticoagulant list (generic + brand, aspirin and clopidogrel
  excluded). A plan for the same drug must have both pre-op and post-op
  instructions.
  Category: ANTICOAGULATION_MANAGEMENT
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Rule: 4. Safety
  Logic: Latest BP and latest temperature from structured + note vitals. ≥180 /
  ≥110 / >100.4 °F (Celsius converted in code).
  Category: ACUTE_SAFETY_EXCLUSION

  Decision: any safety exclusion → NOT_CLEARED; otherwise any issue →
  NEEDS_FOLLOW_UP; otherwise READY. Every rule always runs.

  Evidence

  Each issue and each passed check has rule, finding, computed, and sources[].
  [new] Every source has the same shape, whether it came from a document or a
  structured field:

  {"kind": "document", "id": "c92f…", "date": "2026-02-12", "quote": "…exact
  text…"}
  {"kind": "lab",      "id": "2abe…", "date": "2026-02-11", "quote": "CBC ·
  Hemogram/CBC · final"}

  "quote": "BP 119/73 (Primary care visit)"}
  {"kind": "medication","id": "medications[0]", "quote": "tamsulosin 0.4 mg PO
  nightly (active)"}
  {"kind": "field",    "id": "procedure.procedure_date", "quote": "null"}

  [new] Structured data doesn't always have ids. Labs and documents have real
  ids. Vitals, medications and procedure fields don't, so I'll cite them by
  position or path (vitals[1], medications[0], procedure.procedure_date). For
  structured data, the quote is the raw field values formatted as text. A nurse
  can find any source with this reference.

  Files

  - core.py: shared types and the updated TriageOutput/evidence models.
    triage_submission(submission, *, model) keeps its signature and runs extract
    → verify → rules → output.
  - extraction.py: prompt, schema, OpenAI call.
  - verify.py [new]: normalization, quote check, value check, conversion of
    discarded facts into issues.
  - rules.py: pure policy functions.
  - tests/test_verify.py [new]:
    - accepts a quote that differs only in case, whitespace, line breaks or
      curly vs. straight quotes
    - rejects a paraphrased quote, an unknown doc_id, and an empty quote
    - rejects a real quote paired with a wrong number
    - a discarded fact produces MISSING_REQUIRED_DATA and moves READY to
      NEEDS_FOLLOW_UP
    - a discarded fact can't override a verified NOT_CLEARED
  - tests/test_rules.py: boundary cases (30/31, 14/15, diastolic exactly 110,
    37.9 °C, entered-in-error labs, aspirin only, plan with no resume step, plan
    for the wrong drug, missing date/risk/temperature).
  - tests/test_triage_submission.py: updated for the new flow.

  Build order

❯ do you think this design is scalable in the long run? adding more conditions
  and hard-coded python checks? or would another approach like RAG or something
  similar work better? please explain and justify I am thinking about
  scalibility

⏺ Short answer: yes, this design scales well, as long as the rules aren't
  written as a growing pile of if-statements. RAG would solve a problem this
  system doesn't have, and it would bring back the problem we're trying to
  remove.

  What RAG is for, and why it doesn't fit here

  RAG (retrieval-augmented generation) is useful when the information the model
  needs is too large to send in one prompt. You index a large body of text,
  retrieve the relevant chunks, and give only those to the LLM.

  Here, neither side is large:
  - The patient chart has at most 8 documents and about 7.5k characters, around
    2k tokens. That fits easily in one prompt, so retrieval would only add a way
    to miss things. If the chunk with "38.6 C" isn't retrieved, the fever is
    missed, and that's exactly the kind of error that hurts patients.
  - The policy is one page. There's nothing to search.

  More importantly, RAG is a way of giving an LLM context so it can answer. If
  the LLM is still answering "is this patient READY?", it still makes the
  decision, and the problems we found in the baseline come back: decisions that
  contradict its own reasoning, wrong date math, and invented requirements.
  Better context doesn't fix any of those.

  How the hybrid design behaves as things grow

  What grows: More rules
  Hybrid (LLM extracts, code decides): Each new rule is a small, tested
  function.
  Existing rules can't break silently.
  LLM decides (with or without RAG): The prompt gets longer and every rule
  competes for the model's attention. A new rule can quietly change results on
  old cases.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  What grows: Changed thresholds or windows
  Hybrid (LLM extracts, code decides): Change one config value and re-run the
  rules on cached facts. No LLM cost, and the effect is exactly predictable.
  LLM decides (with or without RAG): Rewrite the prompt, re-run everything, hope

  the model follows it.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  What grows: More sites with different policies
  Hybrid (LLM extracts, code decides): Same engine and extraction, one policy
  config per site.
  LLM decides (with or without RAG): One prompt per site, each with its own
  accuracy problems.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  What grows: More patients
  Hybrid (LLM extracts, code decides): One LLM call per case no matter how many
  rules. Rules take microseconds.
  LLM decides (with or without RAG): Same cost, but accuracy doesn't improve as
  volume grows.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  What grows: Audit and compliance
  Hybrid (LLM extracts, code decides): Every decision traces back to a rule, a
  computed value and a verified quote. Re-running gives the same answer.
  LLM decides (with or without RAG): "The model decided" is hard to defend, and
  results change from run to run.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  What grows: Messier documents
  Hybrid (LLM extracts, code decides): Add fact types or improve the extraction
  prompt. Every fact is still quote-verified.
  LLM decides (with or without RAG): Same work, without the verification safety
  net.

  The work each side does stays narrow: the LLM answers "what does this document
  say?" and code answers "what does the policy say about that?" Adding a rule
  usually means one new fact type and one new function. Rules don't interact
  through a shared prompt.

  Your concern is real, though

  Hardcoded checks become a problem when the policy is spread through branching
  code: thresholds buried in conditions, drug names in several places, rules
  depending on each other's internals. That's how rule systems get
  unmaintainable. To avoid it, I'd make two changes to the plan:

  1. Move the policy into data. A policy.py (or YAML) file holds the parts that
     change: windows (hp_max_days: 30), the required tests per risk level,
     lab-code aliases, BP and temperature thresholds, the anticoagulant list
     with brand names, and the effective date. Most real policy changes ("HIGH
     risk now needs a coagulation panel within 7 days") become a config edit
     that a clinical-ops person could review, not new code.
  2. Make the rules a uniform list. Each rule is a small function with the same
     signature, (facts, policy) -> list[Issue | PassedCheck], collected in one
     registry. The engine runs them all and derives the decision. Adding a rule
     means writing one function, registering it, and adding its tests. Nothing
     else changes.

  A truly new kind of rule (say, "BMI over 40 needs an anesthesia consult")
  still needs code: one new fact type and one function. That's fine. It's a
  reviewed, tested change to safety-critical logic, which is what you'd want
  anyway.

  Where retrieval would make sense later

  If charts grew to hundreds of documents, a cheap pre-filtering step before
  extraction would make sense, for example sending only documents from the last
  60 days or only certain note types. That's retrieval inside the extraction
  step, used to save cost. It doesn't replace the design.

  Something more forward-looking: an LLM could draft policy-config changes from
  a new policy PDF, for a human to review. It would help write the rules, never
  apply them.

  Recommendation

  Keep the hybrid design and add the two changes: a policy config and a rule
  registry. That's maybe 30 minutes of extra structure, and it gives your
  write-up a clear scalability story: policy changes are config diffs, new rules
  are isolated functions, the LLM cost is constant per case, and every decision
  can be audited.

  Shall I add those two changes to the plan? Then I still need your answers on
  the open decisions (A, B and 1–6), or a "go with the recommendations".

⏺ Chunk 1 is ready for review: I added the policy as a data file with two lookup

  The changes:
  - policy.py holds every number and name list from the policy in one object.
    Code that applies the rules (later chunks) will read from it, so a policy
    change means editing this file only.
    - Time limits: an H&P within 30 days; required labs by procedure risk (CBC
      within 30 days for LOW and MODERATE, CBC and CMP within 14 days for HIGH).
      Limits are inclusive, so exactly 30 days passes. That matches the human
      label on case 00012.
    - Safety thresholds: systolic at or above 180, diastolic at or above 110,
      temperature above 100.4 °F.
    - Lab codes: LAB-CBC counts as a CBC and LAB-CMP as a CMP. The BMP codes are
      deliberately left out, so a BMP never counts as a CMP.
    - Excluded lab results: entered-in-error results are ignored. I also
      excluded cancelled, which doesn't appear in the sample data; a cancelled
      lab isn't a real result.
    - Anticoagulants: 9 drugs, each listed under its generic name and its brand
      names. Aspirin and clopidogrel are left out on purpose, because they are
      antiplatelet drugs, not anticoagulants.
  - The two lookup helpers:
    - canonical_lab("LAB-CBC") returns "CBC", and returns None for any lab the
      policy doesn't require.
    - anticoagulant_generic("Xarelto 20 mg PO daily") returns "rivaroxaban". It
      matches whole words, so a drug name hidden inside a longer word can't
      trigger a false match.
  - tests/test_policy.py:
    - every procedure risk level has its lab requirements defined
    - lab code mapping, including that a BMP doesn't count
    - brand and generic drug names are recognised
    - aspirin, ASA and clopidogrel are not treated as anticoagulants

⏺ I removed both pieces and make test still passes (28 tests; one fewer because
  of the deleted test). Here's what remains and why each part is needed.

  policy.py: the policy's numbers and word lists in one place

  Think of it as the settings file for the rules. The code that applies the
  rules (written in a later chunk) never hardcodes "30 days" or "180 mmHg". It
  always looks them up here, so changing the policy means editing this file
  only.

  LabRequirement (lines 13–17) describes one required test, like "CBC, at most
  30 days old". The comment notes the limit is inclusive: exactly 30 days still
  passes, as the human label on case 00012 shows.

  Policy (lines 20–37) lists every setting the rules need, grouped by rule:

  ┌────────────────────────────┬──────┬────────────────────────────────────┐
  │          Setting           │ Used │          Why it's needed           │
  │                            │  by  │                                    │
  ├────────────────────────────┼──────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ hp_max_days                │ Rule │ The H&P must be within 30 days     │
  │                            │  1   │                                    │
  ├────────────────────────────┼──────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ required_labs              │ Rule │ Which tests each risk level needs, │
  │                            │  2   │  and how recent they must be       │
  ├────────────────────────────┼──────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ lab_code_aliases           │ Rule │ The data spells a CBC as both CBC  │
  │                            │  2   │ and LAB-CBC                        │
  ├────────────────────────────┼──────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
  │                            │ Rule │ Results marked entered-in-error    │
  │ excluded_lab_statuses      │  2   │ don't count (cases 00004, 00006,   │
  │                            │      │ 00041)                             │
  ├────────────────────────────┼──────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ anticoagulants             │ Rule │ Recognising blood thinners by      │
  │                            │  3   │ generic or brand name              │
  ├────────────────────────────┼──────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ systolic_hold_at,          │      │ The safety thresholds. The         │
  │ diastolic_hold_at,         │ Rule │ comments say which comparison      │
  │ temp_f_hold_above          │  4   │ applies (≥ or >), because 110 vs.  │
  │                            │      │ "over 110" matters for case 00028. │
  └────────────────────────────┴──────┴────────────────────────────────────┘

  frozen=True stops code from accidentally swapping a setting while the program
  runs, for example setting hp_max_days = 45. One limitation: it doesn't lock
  the contents of the dictionaries inside it, so it's a guard against accidents,
  not a hard lock.

  _ANTICOAGULANT_BRANDS (lines 40–52) lists each generic drug with its brand
  names. The comment explains why aspirin and clopidogrel are missing on
  purpose. The baseline flagged aspirin in 4 failing cases.

  POLICY (lines 54–76) is the actual policy with the real numbers filled in. The
  anticoagulant list is turned into a single lookup table, so "Xarelto",
  "xarelto" and "rivaroxaban" all lead to rivaroxaban.

  Two lookup helpers (lines 79–104):
  - canonical_lab("LAB-CBC") returns "CBC". It returns None for anything the
    policy doesn't require, such as BMP or TSH. It also trims spaces and ignores
    capitals; the sample data is clean, but this costs nothing.
  - anticoagulant_generic("Xarelto 20 mg PO daily") splits the text into words
    and checks each one against the table. Matching whole words means "heparin"
    can't accidentally match inside a longer word.

  Both helpers accept an optional policy argument that defaults to the real one.
  The rules will pass the policy in, so tests or a different site can use
  different settings.

  Things I kept that aren't in the sample data

  You asked for nothing unnecessary, so these three are worth calling out:

  - "cancelled" as an excluded lab status. It never appears in the sample, but
    without it a cancelled CBC would count as a valid test and could wrongly
    clear a patient. It protects against a real failure, so I kept it.
  - Anticoagulants not in the sample: Jantoven, Lixiana, enoxaparin/Lovenox,
    dalteparin, heparin, fondaparinux. The list exists to recognise blood
    thinners in general, not just these 50 patients. A list that only covered
    the sample would quietly miss a real patient on Lovenox.
  - Something to watch for later. Enoxaparin is often mentioned as a temporary
    "bridge" within a warfarin plan (case 00004). If extraction labels that as a
    drug the patient is taking, Rule 3 would wrongly ask for a separate
    enoxaparin plan. I'll handle that in the extraction prompt chunk (only
    extract drugs the patient is currently taking, not drugs mentioned as part
    of a plan), and test it there.

  tests/test_policy.py

  Test: test_every_procedure_risk_has_lab_requirements
  What it protects against: Someone adds a new risk level (say VERY_HIGH)
  without
  saying which labs it needs. The rules would then crash or silently skip the
  check.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Test: test_canonical_lab (10 cases)
  What it protects against: LAB-CBC maps correctly, a BMP never counts as a CMP
  (case 00017), and empty or missing codes don't crash
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Test: test_anticoagulant_generic_recognizes… (8 cases)
  What it protects against: Brand names like Xarelto and Coumadin are caught
  (cases 00000, 00037), including written as "Pradaxa (dabigatran…)"
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Test: test_anticoagulant_generic_ignores… (6 cases)
  What it protects against: Aspirin, ASA and clopidogrel are not treated as
  anticoagulants, which was the baseline's most common mistake

⏺ The branch is deleted locally and on GitHub; only main remains.

  On your three requirements: all of them add value, and I'd keep all three. Two
  need some care in how they're defined, or they'll make results less accurate
  instead of more.

  1. Show passed checks: yes, and make it a guarantee

  This was already partly in the plan (checks_passed), but your version is
  stronger. I'd make it a guarantee that every rule always appears in the
  output, either as an issue or as a passed check, including rules that don't
  apply:

  - ✓ Rule 2: CBC 2026-02-21 (lab 2abe…), 8 days before procedure, limit 30
  - ✓ Rule 3: No active anticoagulant. Med list: tamsulosin, atorvastatin;
    aspirin 81 is an antiplatelet
  - ✓ Rule 4: Latest BP 137/78 at 08:30 (recheck, doc 5f1…) replaced 189/112 at
    08:06

  The last example matters: when code picks between two values, the check shows
  both and says why, so the nurse can see the choice. I'll add a test that every
  output covers all 4 rules plus the missing-data check, so a rule can never be
  silently skipped.

  2. Fail safe: yes, with one refinement

  The quote-failure part is already planned. Your version extends it to every
  failure point:

  ┌─────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │           Failure           │                  Result                   │
  ├─────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Input JSON doesn't match    │ NEEDS_FOLLOW_UP, reason: invalid          │
  │ the expected shape          │ submission                                │
  ├─────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ LLM call fails after        │ NEEDS_FOLLOW_UP, reason: extraction       │
  │ retries or times out        │ unavailable                               │
  ├─────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ LLM returns output that     │ Same as above                             │
  │ doesn't match the schema    │                                           │
  ├─────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ A quote doesn't verify      │ That fact is discarded and flagged; at    │
  │                             │ least NEEDS_FOLLOW_UP                     │
  ├─────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ An unexpected bug in our    │ NEEDS_FOLLOW_UP, reason: internal error,  │
  │ own rules code              │ with the error text included              │
  └─────────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────────────┘

  The refinement: fail-safe should mean the result can never be better than
  NEEDS_FOLLOW_UP. It shouldn't mean the result is always NEEDS_FOLLOW_UP. If
  extraction fails but the structured vitals show BP 190/115, the patient
  shouldn't be downgraded from NOT_CLEARED to NEEDS_FOLLOW_UP just because the
  LLM was down. So on extraction failure:

  - the rules that only use structured data (labs, structured vitals, procedure
    fields) still run
  - the rules that need the notes (H&P, consent, anticoagulation) are reported
    as "not evaluated: extraction failed"
  - the decision is the more severe of NEEDS_FOLLOW_UP and whatever the
    structured rules found

  None of the 5 allowed categories means "system failure", so these issues use
  MISSING_REQUIRED_DATA. I'll add a reason field so the nurse can tell "the
  chart is missing data" apart from "our system couldn't read the chart". Every
  failure path gets a test, including a mocked API error and a mocked garbage
  response.

  3. Flag conflicts: yes, but only real conflicts

  This one needs the most care. Most disagreements in this data aren't
  conflicts: the policy says how to resolve them, and the human labels expect us
  to resolve them:

  ┌────────────────────────────────┬────────────────────────┬───────────────┐
  │          Disagreement          │  Policy resolves it?   │ Label expects │
  ├────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────┼───────────────┤
  │ Older BP 189/112, newer        │ Yes: the most recent   │ READY         │
  │ recheck 137/78 (00016)         │ reading counts         │               │
  ├────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────┼───────────────┤
  │ Pending consent, then a newer  │ Yes: the newer consent │ READY         │
  │ signed one (00007, 00049)      │  replaces it           │               │
  ├────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────┼───────────────┤
  │ CBC later marked               │ Yes: that result       │ READY         │
  │ entered-in-error (00004)       │ doesn't count          │               │
  ├────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────┼───────────────┤
  │ Xarelto missing from the med   │ Not a contradiction:   │ READY,        │
  │ list, but a note says she      │ the note adds          │ treated as on │
  │ takes it (00000)               │ information            │  Xarelto      │
  ├────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────┼───────────────┤
  │ Warfarin marked discontinued,  │ Yes: the switch is     │ The plan must │
  │ note confirms switch to        │ documented             │  be for       │
  │ apixaban (00018)               │                        │ apixaban      │
  └────────────────────────────────┴────────────────────────┴───────────────┘

  If every one of these became an issue, cases labeled READY would turn into
  NEEDS_FOLLOW_UP. Accuracy drops, and nurses learn to ignore the flag. So I'd
  split it in two:

  - Resolvable by a policy rule → not an issue. Code resolves it and the passed
    check (or issue) shows both sources and the reason ("newer recheck
    replaces…"). That still meets your goal: the LLM never silently picks, and
    code picks transparently.
  - Can't be resolved → a separate MISSING_REQUIRED_DATA issue with reason:
    "conflict" and both quotes. Examples:
    - the med list says an anticoagulant is active, but a note says the patient
      stopped it, with no resolution
    - two different anticoagulants both appear active, and neither is marked
      discontinued
    - a note gives a scheduled date different from procedure.procedure_date
    - two readings at the same timestamp with different values

  Schema impact: the procedure-date conflict needs a new extraction fact,
  procedure_date_mentions[] (the date, doc_id and quote whenever a note states
  the scheduled date for this procedure). The risk is false positives from notes
  that mention old or rescheduled dates, so the prompt has to target "the
  scheduled date of this procedure", and I'll count how often it fires.

  A scoring trade-off you should know about: a conflict issue adds
  MISSING_REQUIRED_DATA. If the human labeler didn't flag it, that case fails
  the category check. I think that's the right trade for patient safety, and the
  write-up can say so honestly, but I'll report how many cases it affects.

  What changes in the plan

  - Next chunk (schemas):
    - add procedure_date_mentions[] to the extraction facts
    - output issues and checks share one shape: rule, reason (policy_failed /
      missing / conflict / unverified_quote / extraction_failed / internal_error
      on issues; passed / not_applicable on checks), finding, sources[],
      computed
  - Rules chunk: every rule returns an issue or a passed check. Conflict
    detection sits next to the rule it belongs to. Invariant test: all rules are
    always covered.
  - Wiring chunk: a fail-safe wrapper with a test for each failure path.
  - Write-up: a short "safety design" section covering the fail-safe behavior,
    quote verification, and resolved vs. unresolved conflicts. That's the
    section to walk through in the interview.

  If that matches what you had in mind, I'll start on the schemas chunk.

❯ yes this matches what i had in mind, very well articulated - now can we
  continue implementation of the next chunk, tell me what you are about to do
  and i will approve before making changes

⏺ Here's what the schemas chunk will contain. I won't touch any files until you
  approve.

  Chunk 2: data shapes for facts and output

  This chunk only defines shapes: what the LLM must return, and what our final
  output looks like. There's no API calls, rule logic or quote checking yet.
  Those come in later chunks and build on these shapes.

  File 1 (new): facts.py, what the LLM extracts

  This is a new file, not part of the earlier plan. The fact shapes are used by
  the extraction, the quote check and the rules, so a separate file means the
  quote check and rules don't depend on OpenAI code.

  Every fact has a required doc_id and a required quote. Each field also gets a
  short description, which OpenAI sends to the model as part of the schema.
  Those descriptions are effectively part of the prompt, so the extraction
  guidance lives right next to the fields it applies to.

  Fact type: HPDocument
  Fields (plus doc_id, quote): is_preop_hp: bool
  Notes: True only for a pre-op H&P for this procedure; false for wellness or
  establish-care visits
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Fact type: Consent
  Fields (plus doc_id, quote): patient_signed: bool, procedure_named: str |
  None,
  matches_scheduled_procedure: bool
  Notes: A pending or unsigned consent means patient_signed = false
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Fact type: MedicationMention
  Fields (plus doc_id, quote): name: str, status: "taking" | "stopped" |
  "uncertain"
  Notes: Description says: only drugs the patient takes, not drugs planned as
  part of a bridge (the enoxaparin issue from chunk 1)
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Fact type: AnticoagPlan
  Fields (plus doc_id, quote): drug: str, pre_op_quote: str | None,
  post_op_quote: str | None
  Notes: Two quotes, verified separately. "To be addressed later" doesn't count
  as an instruction.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Fact type: NoteVital
  Fields (plus doc_id, quote): kind: "blood_pressure" | "temperature", systolic,

  diastolic, temperature, temperature_unit: "F" | "C" | None, measured_on: date
   | None, measured_time: "HH:MM" | None
  Notes: Raw numbers and units only. Code converts Celsius to Fahrenheit.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Fact type: ProcedureDateMention
  Fields (plus doc_id, quote): date
  Notes: Only when a note states the scheduled date of this procedure (for
  conflict detection)

  ExtractedFacts holds a list of each type.

  Deliberate choice: H&P and consent dates come from the document's own date
  field (read by code), not from the LLM. That's one less thing the LLM can get
  wrong. A vital's date is the one exception: a note can mention a reading from
  an earlier visit, so the LLM reports measured_on when the note states it, and
  otherwise code uses the document's date.

  All fields are required (optional ones are nullable instead of having
  defaults), and no extra fields are allowed. That's what OpenAI's strict: True
  mode requires.

  File 2 (changed): core.py, the output shape

  This replaces the current TriageIssue(category, evidence: str).

  Source       kind: "document" | "lab" | "vital" | "medication" | "field"
               id: str            # doc_id / lab id / "vitals[1]" /
  "procedure.procedure_date"
               date: str | None
               quote: str

  RuleId       "required_fields" | "hp" | "consent" | "labs" | "anticoagulation"
               | "blood_pressure" | "temperature" | "system"

  _Finding     rule: RuleId, finding: str, sources: list[Source], computed: dict

  TriageIssue  (_Finding) + category: IssueCategory
               + reason: "policy_failed" | "missing" | "conflict" |
  "unverified_quote"
                         | "extraction_failed" | "invalid_submission" |
  "internal_error"

  RuleCheck    (_Finding) + status: "passed" | "not_applicable" |
  "not_evaluated"

  TriageOutput decision, issues: list[TriageIssue], checks: list[RuleCheck],
  explanation

  Notes on these choices:

  - RuleId is a fixed list. The "every rule always appears" test needs a known
    set of rules to check against. system is only used for failure issues
    (extraction failed, internal error).
  - New check status: not_evaluated. If the procedure date is missing, the H&P
    30-day check can't run. It shows up as not_evaluated with the reason, so the
    rule is never silently dropped, and the blocking issue is listed separately
    under required_fields.
  - computed holds the arithmetic for the nurse, for example {"days_before": 31,
    "limit_days": 30}.
  - checks defaults to an empty list for now. That keeps the current baseline
    and its 3 existing tests working until the wiring chunk replaces the
    LLM-decides path.

  File 3 (new): tests/test_schemas.py

  - Every fact type has doc_id and quote fields (enforces your requirement)
  - The ExtractedFacts JSON schema is strict-compatible: every object lists all
    its properties as required and forbids extra properties. This catches a bad
    field before it causes an API error.
  - A full TriageOutput with an issue, a check and sources validates and
    round-trips through JSON
  - Invalid values are rejected: an unknown category, reason, rule or check
    status

  Not in this chunk (noted so nothing is lost)

  - view_report.py expects issues with description and evidence text, so with
    the new shape the issue list in the TUI will show blank text until it's
    updated. I'll update it in the wiring chunk so it shows finding, the sources
    and the passed checks.
  - The prompt text, the API call, the quote check and the rules each come in
    their own chunks.

  The proposed commit message is feat: add extraction fact and evidence output
  schemas. Reply "approve" (or with changes) and I'll write it, run the tests
  and show you the result before committing.

⏺ Chunk 2 is written and tested. Nothing is committed yet. make test passes all

  What was added

  facts.py (new): what the LLM is allowed to return
  - _Fact is the shared base with the two required fields, doc_id and quote.
    Every fact type inherits it, so your requirement is enforced by the
    structure itself, not by convention.
  - Six fact types: HPDocument, Consent, MedicationMention, AnticoagPlan,
    NoteVital, ProcedureDateMention.
  - Every field contains raw observations, never conclusions. For example
    NoteVital holds temperature: 38.6, unit: "C", not "has fever". Code does the
    conversion and the comparison.
  - The description text on each field is extraction guidance OpenAI sends to
    the model, so the instructions sit next to the field they describe. For
    example, MedicationMention says to exclude drugs only proposed in a plan
    (the enoxaparin-bridge issue), and NoteVital says "see flowsheet" isn't a
    reading.
  - ExtractedFacts is the full response: one list per fact type.

  core.py (changed): the output shape
  - TriageIssue used to be just category plus a free-text evidence string. It
    now has category, reason, rule, finding, sources[] and computed.
  - RuleCheck (new) is the shape for passed checks, with status set to passed,
    not_applicable or not_evaluated.
  - Source (new) says where each piece of evidence came from: the kind, the id
    or path, the date and the quote.
  - TriageOutput gained checks. It defaults to an empty list, so the current
    baseline and its 3 tests keep working until the wiring chunk.

  tests/test_schemas.py (new)
  - ExtractedFacts includes every fact type, so a fact type can't be added and
    then forgotten.
  - Every fact type requires doc_id and quote.
  - The schema meets strict mode's requirements: all fields required, no extra
    fields allowed, no default values.
  - A complete output survives a round trip through JSON unchanged.
  - Unknown values are rejected: category, reason, rule, source kind and check
    status.

  Three small differences from what you approved

  1. AnticoagPlan also has the base quote, holding the text that names the drug,
     in addition to the separate before-surgery and after-surgery quotes. The
     plan as I described it only had the two instruction quotes, which would
     have broken "every fact has a quote". This way the drug name gets verified
     too.
  2. There's no shared _Finding base class. With inheritance, Pydantic puts the
     base class's fields first, so issue JSON would start with rule and finding
     and category would come last. I defined both classes directly so a nurse
     reads category and reason first. The cost is 4 repeated field lines.
  3. MedicationMention extracts every medication a document mentions, not just
     anticoagulants. Deciding which drugs count as anticoagulants stays in
     Python (anticoagulant_generic from chunk 1), so the LLM makes no
     classification decisions.

  Checking for unnecessary code

  a rule in the plan, and every test protects against a specific mistake. The
  only part that's temporary on purpose is the checks default, which goes away
  when the baseline path is replaced.
